Remark G.4
.

We will prove in
Proposition

G.6
that the assumption of
Corollary

G.3
is satisfied. Although the first inequality of
Corollary

G.3
already provides bounds on the change of the individual weights, the second inequality is interesting as well because its bounds are stronger than what one would expect only from the individual weight bounds in the first inequality: For the sake of simplicity, pretend that
\varepsilon=\gamma_{\psi}=0
. Then, for example, one could argue using the first inequality that

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\sigma,a^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq l\leq k}\left|\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}(a_{i,l}^{2}-a_{i,0}^{2})\right|\leq\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}\max_{0\leq l\leq k}|a_{i,l}^{2}-a_{i,0}^{2}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}\max_{0\leq l\leq k}|a_{i,l}+a_{i,0}|\cdot|a_{i,l}-a_{i,0}|\leq\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}(|a_{i,0}|+\Delta_{k}a_{i})\Delta_{k}a_{i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{\sigma}}(O(1)+O(m^{-3/2}))O(m^{-3/2})=|I_{\sigma}|O(m^{-3/2})\leq O(m^{-1/2})\penalty\ ,

which is weaker than the bound
\Delta_{k}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\sigma,a^{2}}\leq O(m^{-1})
obtained by the second inequality. These stronger bounds will be crucial in proving that the assumption
\kappa_{u,k}\leq O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})
of
Corollary

G.3
is satisfied. Also, note that for
\varepsilon=\gamma_{\psi}=0
, the weakest bound

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,wa}\leq O(1)

cannot be improved:
\Sigma_{\sigma,wa}=p_{\sigma}
is the slope of
f_{W,\boldsymbol{\tau},\sigma}
, which initially satisfies
|\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,0}|\leq O(1)
by (W7) and needs to converge to an
p^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\sigma}
that is independent of
\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,0}
and also satisfies
|p^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\sigma}|\leq\psi_{D,p}\leq O(1)
. Our proof works since
\Sigma_{\sigma,wa}
only occurs in
h\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{wab}}
with a small factor
h
, but neither in
\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}
nor
\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}
.

We will soon use
Corollary

G.3
to prove its own assumption
\kappa_{u,k}\leq O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})
. To this end, we first need a lemma that connects the reference system
\delta\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
to the actual system
\delta\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=-h\boldsymbol{A}\mathbf{M}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
.

Lemma G.5
.

For
m\geq 1
, let
\|\cdot\|
denote an arbitrary vector norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and its induced matrix norm. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{K}_{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{K}_{k-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
. If

\displaystyle\delta_{k-1}\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\|\cdot\sup_{l\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}}\|\boldsymbol{K}_{l}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}\|<1\penalty\ ,

where
\delta_{-1}\coloneqq 0
, then each sequence
\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\boldsymbol{v}_{l+1}=\boldsymbol{K}_{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{l}
for all
l\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{l}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\delta_{k-1}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|\penalty\ .

Proof

Clearly, for
l\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{l+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}\boldsymbol{v}_{l}+(\boldsymbol{K}_{l}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}})\boldsymbol{v}_{l}

and hence, by induction on
l
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{l-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l-1-l^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{K}_{l^{\prime}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}})\boldsymbol{v}_{l^{\prime}}

for all
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
. Summing norms on both sides yields

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{l}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|+\sum_{l=0}^{k}\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{l-1}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l-1-l^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{K}_{l^{\prime}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}})\boldsymbol{v}_{l^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|+\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\sum_{l=l^{\prime}+1}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l-1-l^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{K}_{l^{\prime}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}})\boldsymbol{v}_{l^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|+\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{k-1-l^{\prime}}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\|\right)\cdot\sup_{l\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}}\|\boldsymbol{K}_{l}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}\|\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}_{l^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|+\delta_{k-1}\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{l^{\prime}}\|\penalty\ .

Hence
(1-\delta_{k-1})\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{l}\|\leq\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}^{l}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|
and since
\delta_{k-1}<1
, the inequality is preserved when dividing by
1-\delta_{k-1}
.
∎

Proposition G.6
.

Let
F.2
be satisfied. We have

\displaystyle\kappa_{u,k}\leq O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ ,

where
\kappa_{u,k}
was defined in
Definition

G.1
and the bound
O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})
is independent of
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof

By
Proposition

C.5
, we know that
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{k+1}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}_{k}\mathbf{M}_{D})\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{k}
. We want to bound
\kappa_{u,k}=h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\|_{\infty}=h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{B}\mathbf{M}_{D}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{l}\|_{\infty}
by comparing it to the reference system
\delta\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}
using
Lemma

G.5
. Hence, we define

\displaystyle\delta_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\|_{\infty}\cdot\sup_{0\leq l\leq k}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})-(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}_{l}\mathbf{M}_{D})\|_{\infty}

(17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|(I-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\|_{\infty}\cdot\sup_{0\leq l\leq k}\|(\boldsymbol{A}_{l}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}\penalty\ .

For
m
large enough, we want to prove by induction that
\delta_{k}\leq 1/2
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Trivially,
\delta_{-1}=0\leq 1/2
. Now let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
\delta_{k-1}\leq 1/2
.

1.

By
Lemma

C.4
, we have
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{k}=-\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{D}\tilde{\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}}_{k}
and hence
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=-\boldsymbol{B}\mathbf{M}_{D}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{k}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\kappa_{u,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}\cdot h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{l}\|_{\infty}\penalty\ .

Because
\delta_{k-1}\leq 1/2
, we can apply
Lemma

G.5
and obtain

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{l}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\delta_{k-1}}h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2h\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ref_sum}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

Norm equivalence (cf.
Definition

A.3
) yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}\leq O(\|\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{2})=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{M}_{D}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{assumption:training_dynamics}}}}{{=}}O(1)\penalty\ .

(18)

Hence, we can write

\displaystyle\kappa_{u,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ ,

(19)

where, in accordance with
Definition

A.2
, the constant in
O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})
does not depend on the induction step
k
.

2.

Let us investigate the components of Eq. (
17
):

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\|_{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ref_sum}}}}{{=}}O(1)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{A}_{l}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})\mathbf{M}_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}\left((\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{0})+(\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{0})+h\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{wab}}_{l}\right)\boldsymbol{B}\mathbf{M}_{D}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\|(\boldsymbol{A}_{l}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:norm-M_D-is-bounded}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle O(1)\cdot(\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{0}\|_{\infty}+\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{0}\|_{\infty}+h\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{wab}}_{l}\|_{\infty}).

First of all, for
0\leq l\leq k
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{w}}_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,w^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:difference_bound_3}}}}{{\leq}}O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

Similarly, for
0\leq l\leq k
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{l}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{ab}}_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,a^{2}}+\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,ab}+\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,b^{2}})\leq O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

Observe that

\displaystyle h|r_{\sigma,l}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle h\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\|_{\infty}\leq h\sum_{l^{\prime}=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}=\kappa_{u,k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa_u_k-is-small}}}{{=}}O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})

and similarly
h|s_{\sigma,l}|\leq O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})
. Thus, we find

\displaystyle h\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{wab}}_{l}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}h|r_{\sigma,l}\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,l}+s_{\sigma,l}\Sigma_{\sigma,wb,l}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\cdot\left(\max_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}|\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,l}|+|\Sigma_{\sigma,wb,l}|\right)\penalty\ .

Similar to the other calculations, we can compute for
0\leq l\leq k

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,l}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{\sigma,wa,0}|+\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,wa}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(W7)}}}{{\leq}}O(m^{\varepsilon})+O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}})=O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}})

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{\sigma,wb,l}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{\sigma,wb,0}|+\Delta_{k}\Sigma_{\sigma,wb}\leq 0+O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})=O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ ,

which yields
h\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\mathrm{wab}}_{l}\|_{\infty}\leq O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})
.

We can now revisit the beginning of step (2) to obtain

\displaystyle\|(\boldsymbol{A}_{l}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}\leq O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})

and

\displaystyle\delta_{k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:def_delta_k}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{4}-h\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D})^{l}\|_{\infty}\cdot\sup_{0\leq l\leq k}\|(\boldsymbol{A}_{l}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})\mathbf{M}_{D}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1)\cdot O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})=O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

We have shown that
\delta_{k-1}\leq 1/2
implies
\delta_{k}\leq O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})
, where the constant in
O(m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1})
does not depend on
k
. Since
3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}<1
by
F.2
, we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}m^{3\varepsilon+2\gamma_{\psi}-1}=0
and there exists
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\delta_{k-1}\leq 1/2
implies
\delta_{k}\leq 1/2
and the induction works.
Thus, for all
m\geq m_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we know that
\delta_{k-1}\leq 1/2
and we can apply step (1) to obtain

\displaystyle\kappa_{u,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(m^{\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

∎

We can now prove our main theorem:

Proof of
Theorem

10

Since
\gamma_{\psi}+\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}+\gamma_{P}<1/2
, we can assume without loss of generality that

\displaystyle 0<\varepsilon<\frac{1/2-(\gamma_{\psi}+\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}+\gamma_{P})}{3}\penalty\ .

Moreover, if (W1) – (W7) from
Proposition

E.3
are satisfied, we have (similar to Eq. (
12
) in the proof of
Proposition

F.4
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{M}_{D}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}}\mathbf{M}_{D}^{1/2})\leq\|\mathbf{M}_{D}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})=\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{M}_{D})\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\mathrm{ref}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:eig_Aref}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle K_{M}\Theta(m)\penalty\ .

Hence, there exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{lr}}^{-1}>0
with
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{H})\leq C_{\mathrm{lr}}^{-1}m
. For this choice, assumption (
3
) yields

\displaystyle h\leq C_{\mathrm{lr}}m^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{H})}\penalty\ .

Therefore,
F.2
is satisfied whenever the initialization satisfies (W1) – (W7).

By our choice of
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-3/2

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle-1-\gamma_{P}-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}\leq-1-\gamma_{P}\penalty\ .

(20)

For
\underline{x}\coloneqq K_{\mathrm{data}}m^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}}
, we then obtain using (W4),
Corollary

G.3
and
Proposition

G.6
:

\displaystyle(|a_{i,0}|-|a_{i,0}-a_{i,k}|)\underline{x}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\Omega(m^{-1-\gamma_{P}})-O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-3/2})\right)\Theta(m^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:eps}}}{{=}}\Omega(m^{-1-\gamma_{P}-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}})

\displaystyle|b_{i,k}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|b_{i,k}-b_{i,0}|\leq O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-3/2})\penalty\ .

Using (
20
) again, we find that there exists
m_{0}
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}
and all
i\in I,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle|b_{i,k}|<(|a_{i,0}|-|a_{i,0}-a_{i,k}|)\underline{x}\penalty\ ,

which, in terms of
Lemma

B.2
, means
W_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{W_{0}}(\underline{x})
. Since
\underline{x}\leq\underline{x}_{D}
by assumption, we also have
W_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{W_{0}}(\underline{x}_{D})
. But then,
Lemma

B.2
tells us that
\nabla L_{D,\boldsymbol{\tau}}(W_{k})=\nabla L_{D}(W_{k})
and that
f_{W}|_{[\underline{x},\infty)}=f_{W,\boldsymbol{\tau},1}|_{[\underline{x},\infty)}
as well as
f_{W}|_{(-\infty,-\underline{x}]}=f_{W,\boldsymbol{\tau},-1}|_{(-\infty,-\underline{x}]}
are affine. Hence,
(W_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies the original gradient descent iteration

\displaystyle W_{k+1}=W_{k}-h\nabla L_{D}(W_{k})

and (v) is satisfied. Moreover, by
Corollary

G.3
, we obtain (i), (ii) and (iii) (up to a factor
2
in front of
\varepsilon
, which can be resolved by shrinking
\varepsilon
):

\displaystyle|a_{i,k}-a_{i,0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-3/2})

\displaystyle|b_{i,k}-b_{i,0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-3/2})

\displaystyle|w_{i,k}-w_{i,0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ .

In order to find a similar bound for
c
, we recall from
Lemma

C.4
that
\delta c=h(\hat{s}_{1}+\hat{s}_{-1})
, from
Definition

G.1
that
\kappa_{u,k}=h\sum_{l=0}^{k}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\|_{\infty}
and from
Definition

C.1
that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{r}_{1}\\
\hat{r}_{-1}\\
\hat{s}_{1}\\
\hat{s}_{-1}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and therefore }\quad\begin{pmatrix}\hat{r}_{1}\\
\hat{r}_{-1}\\
\hat{s}_{1}\\
\hat{s}_{-1}\end{pmatrix}=\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}\penalty\ ,

since
\boldsymbol{B}
and invertible due to
|\alpha|\neq 1
. Because
\boldsymbol{B}
is fixed, we therefore obtain

\displaystyle|c_{k}-c_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}|c_{l+1}-c_{l}|=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}|\delta c_{l}|=h\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}|\hat{s}_{1,l}+\hat{s}_{-1,l}|\leq O(\kappa_{u,k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:sum-O}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle O(m^{2\varepsilon+\gamma_{\psi}-1})\penalty\ ,

which shows (iv) after rescaling
\varepsilon
.

All of this holds under the assumption that
m\geq m_{0}
and (W1) – (W7), where
m_{0}
is independent of
W_{0},k,h
. By
Proposition

E.3
, the assumption holds with probability
\geq 1-O(m^{-\gamma_{P}})
.
∎

Appendix H
Multi-Dimensional Inputs

In the following, we investigate the case where

•

the one-dimensional
x
values of
D
are projected onto a line in a
d
-dimensional input space (
d\in\mathbb{N}
), and

•

a two-layer neural network with
d
-dimensional input is trained on this (degenerate) data set.

In
Remark

H.1
, we show that this
d
-dimensional case can be reduced to the case
d=1
and hence, comparable conclusions hold:

Remark H.1
.

Let
d\geq 1
and let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{2}=1
. For a data set
D\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}
, we consider an embedded data set
\tilde{D}=((\boldsymbol{z}x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\boldsymbol{z}x_{n},y_{n}))\in(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}
and a neural network function

\displaystyle f_{\tilde{W}}(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\tilde{c}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{w}_{i}\varphi(\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}+\tilde{b}_{i})\penalty\ .

Then,

\displaystyle f_{\tilde{W}}(\boldsymbol{z}x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\tilde{c}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{w}_{i}\varphi(\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}x+\tilde{b}_{i})=\tilde{c}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{w}_{i}\varphi((\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i})x+\tilde{b}_{i})=f_{\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}}(x)\penalty\ ,

(21)

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\\
&\ddots\\
&&\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\\
&&&\boldsymbol{I}_{m}\\
&&&&1\\
&&&&&\boldsymbol{I}_{m}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{m}\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{b}}\\
\tilde{c}\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{m}\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{b}}\\
\tilde{c}\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{3m+1}\penalty\ .

If we naturally extend the definition of
L_{D}
in the usual way for
d
-dimensional inputs, Eq. (
21
) yields
L_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{W})=L_{D}(\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W})
. Moreover, since
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{2}=1
, we have
\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{I}_{3m+1}
. Using these two insights, we obtain for
W_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k}
:

\displaystyle W_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k+1}=\boldsymbol{Z}(\tilde{W}_{k}-h\nabla_{\tilde{W}_{k}}L_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{W}_{k}))=\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k}-h\boldsymbol{Z}\nabla_{\tilde{W}_{k}}L_{D}(\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k}-h\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\nabla L_{D}(\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k})=\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k}-h\nabla L_{D}(\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k})=W_{k}-h\nabla L_{D}(W_{k})\penalty\ .

Hence,
(W_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfy the gradient descent equation for the original data set
D
. Moreover, if we initialize
\tilde{W}_{0}
analogous to
2
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\quad\tilde{c}=0,\quad\tilde{w}_{i}\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\tilde{Z}_{\mathrm{w}},\quad\tilde{a}_{il}\sim\tilde{Z}_{\mathrm{a}}

(22)

with independent variables, then the initial vector
\tilde{W}_{0}=\boldsymbol{Z}W_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{i}=0,\quad c=\tilde{c}=0,\quad w_{i}=\tilde{w}_{i}\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\tilde{Z}_{\mathrm{w}},\quad a_{i}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}z_{l}\tilde{a}_{il}\sim Z_{\mathrm{a}}

with independent variables and suitable
Z_{\mathrm{a}}
. The random variables
(Z_{\mathrm{a}},\tilde{Z}_{\mathrm{w}})
satisfy (Q1) and (Q2) from
2
(we only need to verify them for
Z_{\mathrm{a}}
):

1.

It is well-known that the sum of independent
\mathbb{R}
-valued random-variables
X,Y
with densities
p_{X},p_{Y}
has density

\displaystyle p_{X+Y}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{X}(x-y)p_{Y}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\penalty\ .

Hence, if we know that there exists a bound
B\in(0,\infty)
with
p_{X}(x)\leq B
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\displaystyle p_{X+Y}(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle B\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}p_{Y}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=B\penalty\ .

Moreover, if
p_{X}
and
p_{Y}
are symmetric, then
p_{X+Y}
is also symmetric:

\displaystyle p_{X+Y}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{X}(x-y)p_{Y}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{X}(y-x)p_{Y}(-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y^{\prime}\coloneqq-y}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{X}((-x)-y^{\prime})p_{Y}(y^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}=p_{X+Y}(-x)\penalty\ .

This directly yields (Q1) for
Z_{\mathrm{a}}
.

2.

Since
Z_{\mathrm{a}}
can be written as a linear combination of random variables that satisfy (Q2),
Z_{\mathrm{a}}
must also satisfy (Q2) by the Minkowski inequality.

By Eq. (
21
), we obtain

\displaystyle f_{\tilde{W}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{z}x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{W_{k}}(x)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Especially, we can apply Theorems
10
,
12
and
24
and obtain that under the assumptions of these theorems, the kinks of
x\mapsto f_{\tilde{W}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{z}x)
do not cross the data points with high probability.

Since the assumptions of the theorems are (up to modifying constants) invariant under multiplying the
x_{j}
by a positive constant, we can also allow
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{2}\neq 1
as long as
\boldsymbol{z}\neq 0
.

Appendix I
Inconsistency Proofs

In this section, we give proofs of the inconsistency results in
Section

6
.

Proof of
Corollary

26

Let
\gamma_{\psi}\in(0,1/2)
be sufficiently large such that
\frac{1}{2-2\gamma_{\psi}}\geq 1-\varepsilon
. We can then choose
K_{\mathrm{param}}>0
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle m_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K_{\mathrm{param}}n^{\frac{1}{2-2\gamma_{\psi}}}

for all
n\geq 1
. Choose
K_{\mathrm{data}}>0
such that
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}([-K_{\mathrm{data}},K_{\mathrm{data}}])=0
. Moreover, let
\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}=0
and
\gamma_{P}>0
such that
\gamma_{P}+\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}+\gamma_{\psi}<1/2
.

Let
C_{\mathrm{lr}}
be the corresponding constant from
Theorem

24
. Since
m_{n}\to\infty
and
h_{n}<o(m_{n}^{-1})
, there exists an
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{lr}}m_{n}^{-1}\penalty\ .

By
Theorem

24
we hence obtain for all
n\geq n_{0}
that
f_{W_{k}}
is affine on
(-\infty,-K_{\mathrm{data}}]
and
[K_{\mathrm{data}},\infty)
with probability
\geq 1-C_{P}m_{n}^{-\gamma_{P}}\to 1\quad(n\to\infty)
. But such a function satisfies
f_{W_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}_{K_{\mathrm{data}}}
and, because
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}([-K_{\mathrm{data}},K_{\mathrm{data}}])=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f_{W_{k}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},K_{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}=R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(P4)}}}{{>}}R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}\penalty\ ,

which yields inconsistency.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

28

Consider an NN as in
Corollary

26

that is inconsistent on a distribution
P^{\mathrm{data}}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore,
fix an arbitrary vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{2}=1
.

For
(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}
, let
\tilde{P}^{\mathrm{data}}
denote the distribution of
(x\boldsymbol{z},y)
. It is easy to show that the optimal population risks satisfy

\displaystyle R_{\tilde{P}^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}=R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}\penalty\ .

(23)

Let
D\sim(P^{\mathrm{data}})^{n}
, i.e.,
D
consists of
n
i.i.d. data points
(x_{j},y_{j})\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}
, then
\tilde{D}\sim(\tilde{P}^{\mathrm{data}})^{n}
with
\tilde{D}
defined in
Remark

H.1
. Let
\tilde{W}_{0}
be independent from
\tilde{D}
and initialized analogous to
2
as discussed in
Remark

H.1
. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{k}-h_{n}\nabla L_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{W}_{k})\penalty\ .

Let
W_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{Z}\tilde{W}_{k}
. As shown in
Remark

H.1
,
W_{0}
satisfies
2
and
(W_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
arises from gradient descent on
D
:

\displaystyle W_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{k}-h_{n}\nabla L_{D}(W_{k})\penalty\ .

Moreover,
f_{\tilde{W}_{k}}(x\boldsymbol{z})=f_{W_{k}}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and therefore

\displaystyle R_{\tilde{P}^{\mathrm{data}}}(f_{\tilde{W}_{k}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f_{W_{k}})

(24)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Since the NN with one-dimensional input is inconsistent on
P^{\mathrm{data}}
, the NN with
d
-dimensional input must be inconsistent on
\tilde{P}^{\mathrm{data}}
by (
23
) and (
24
).
∎

In order to prove
Corollary

27
, we first show that if functions that are affine on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
cannot approach the Bayes risk
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that the same holds for functions that are affine on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-\delta,\delta]
.

Lemma I.1
.

Let
P^{\mathrm{data}}
be a bounded distribution on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfying (P1). For
\delta\geq 0
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\coloneqq\{f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\mid f\text{ affine on }(-\infty,-\delta)\text{ and }(\delta,\infty)\},\quad R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}\coloneqq\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}}R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)\penalty\ .

Then,
\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}=R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}
.

Proof

In the case
\eta=\infty
, we obviously have
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}=R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}
for all
\delta\geq 0
with
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}((-\delta,\delta))=0
and we are done.

Obviously,
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}
is non-increasing in
\delta
. In order to derive a contradiction, assume that
\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}<R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}
.

For
\delta^{\prime}>0,\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
, consider
P_{\sigma}\coloneqq P^{\mathrm{data}}(\cdot\mid\sigma X>\delta^{\prime})
. For sufficiently small
\delta^{\prime}
,
P
is well-defined and
P_{X}
is not only concentrated on a single
x
value due to (P1). Now, fix such a
\delta^{\prime}>0
.

For
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
, define
\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
as the slope and intercept of
f
on
\sigma(\delta,\infty)
. As in
Definition

8
, we can construct an invertible matrix
\mathbf{M}_{P_{\sigma}}=\mathbf{M}_{P_{\sigma},\sigma}
and a vector
\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma}}=\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma},\sigma}
. Analogous to
Remark

D.6
, we obtain for
\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle R_{P}(f)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)-\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma}})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{P_{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)-\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma}})\penalty\ .

Hence, for
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
with
\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
and
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)\leq R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}}\mathds{1}_{\sigma(\delta^{\prime},\infty)}(x)(y-f(x))^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}(\sigma(\delta^{\prime},\infty))\cdot\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P_{\sigma}}(y-f(x))^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}(\sigma(\delta^{\prime},\infty))\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)-\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma}})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{P_{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)-\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P_{\sigma}})\penalty\ .

Since
\mathbf{M}_{P_{\sigma}}\succ 0
and
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}(\sigma(\delta^{\prime},\infty))>0
, there has to exist a constant
C
such that
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}(f)\|_{\infty}\geq C
for all such
f
and
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
.

Now, pick
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
,
0<\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
, with
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)\leq R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}
and let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{hsal}}
be its affine continuation (i.e.,
f_{0}(x)=f(x)
for
|x|>\delta
). Then,
|f_{0}(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)
for
x>0
and therefore

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}}\mathds{1}_{[-\delta,\delta]}(x)(y-f_{0}(x))^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}}\mathds{1}_{[-\delta,\delta]^{c}}(x)(y-f_{0}(x))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}}\mathds{1}_{[-\delta,\delta]}(x)2(y^{2}+f_{0}(x)^{2})+R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim P^{\mathrm{data}}}\mathds{1}_{[-\delta,\delta]}(x)y^{2}}_{\to 0\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta\to 0)}+\underbrace{P^{\mathrm{data}}_{X}([-\delta,\delta])\cdot(C(1+|\delta|))^{2}}_{\to 0\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta\to 0)}+R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)\penalty\ .

Since we can choose
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f)
arbitrarily close to
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}
, it follows that
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}\leq\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}
, which contradicts our initial assumption.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

27

Since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma\eta}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{2\gamma}+\frac{1}{\eta\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1-\frac{2}{\eta}}{2\gamma}=\frac{2\gamma-\left(1-\frac{2}{\eta}\right)}{2\gamma}<\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ ,

there exist some

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,1-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma\eta}

\displaystyle\gamma_{P}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0

such that
\gamma_{\psi}+\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}+\gamma_{P}<1/2
. We then have

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2-2\gamma_{\psi}}

\displaystyle 1-\gamma\eta\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 0

and therefore

\displaystyle m_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O\left(n^{\frac{1}{2-2\gamma_{\psi}}}\right)

\displaystyle O(nm_{n}^{-\eta\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(1)

\displaystyle O(m_{n}^{-\gamma_{P}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(1)\penalty\ .

Let
C_{\mathrm{lr}}
be the corresponding constant from
Theorem

24
. Since
m_{n}\to\infty
and
h_{n}<o(m_{n}^{-1})
, there exists an
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{lr}}m_{n}^{-1}\penalty\ .

Hence, by
Theorem

24
, we obtain for all
n\geq n_{0}
that
f_{W_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}_{K_{\mathrm{data}}m_{n}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}}}
with probability
\geq 1-C_{P}(m_{n}^{-\gamma_{P}}+nm_{n}^{-\eta\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}})\to 1
for
n\to\infty
. By assumption (P4), we have
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},0}^{*}>R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}
and by
Lemma

I.1
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}>R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}
. For
n
sufficiently large, we have
K_{\mathrm{data}}m_{n}^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}}\leq\delta
and therefore

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}(f_{W_{k}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle R_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\delta}^{*}>R_{P^{\mathrm{data}}}^{*}

with probability
\geq 1-C_{P}(m_{n}^{-\gamma_{P}}+nm_{n}^{-\eta\gamma_{\mathrm{data}}})\to 1
for
n\to\infty
. This shows inconsistency.
∎

Appendix J
Miscellaneous

In this section, we prove a fact that has been mentioned in the main paper.

Lemma J.1
.

Let
D=((x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n}))\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}
with
n\geq 1
and
x_{j}\neq 0
for all
j
. Then, by adding three points to
D
, we can achieve that
\mathbf{M}_{D,\sigma}
is invertible for both
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
and that
\psi_{D,q}=0
.

Proof

By adding at most one point to
D
, we can ensure that both
D_{1}
and
D_{-1}
are nonempty. Now consider the case of
D_{1}
(
D_{-1}
can be handled analogously). For
x^{\prime}\coloneqq 1+\max_{(x,y)\in D_{1}}x
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
yet to be specified, consider the data set
\tilde{D}\coloneqq D\cup\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\}
. Since the kernels of two different matrices
\mathbf{M}_{x_{j}}\succeq 0
and
\mathbf{M}_{x^{\prime}}\succeq 0
only intersect in zero, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{x^{\prime}}+\sum_{(x,y)\in D_{1}}\mathbf{M}_{x}\right)\succ 0\penalty\ ,

i.e.,
\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}
is invertible. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\tilde{D},1}\\
q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\tilde{D},1}\end{pmatrix}=\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\tilde{D},1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{\tilde{D},1}=\frac{1}{n+1}\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}+\sum_{(x,y)\in D_{1}}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{(x,y)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{y^{\prime}}{n+1}\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix}+\frac{n}{n+1}\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{D,1}

We need to show that we can choose
y^{\prime}
such that
q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\tilde{D},1}=0
. Assume the contrary, which means that there exists
z\in\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{M}_{\tilde{D},1}\begin{pmatrix}z^{\prime}\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{z^{\prime}}{n+1}\left(\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{(x,y)\in D_{1}}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)\penalty\ .

Since
D_{1}
is nonempty by assumption and all
(x,y)\in D_{1}
satisfy
x<x^{\prime}
, we obtain the desired contradiction.

Overall, we can therefore satisfy
M_{D,\sigma}\succ 0
for both
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
by adding at most point to
D
and we can then satisfy
\psi_{D,q}=0
by adding at most two more points to
D
.
∎

Appendix K
NTK Relation Proofs

Proposition K.1
.

Let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{3m+1}
be a parameter vector that induces the same activation pattern as
W_{0}
on
D
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sgn(a_{i}x_{j}+b_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sgn(a_{i,0}x_{j}+b_{i,0})

for all
i\in I,j\in J
, such that
f_{W,\tau,\sgn(x_{j})}(x_{j})=f_{W}(x_{j})
for all
j\in J
. By the proof of
Lemma

B.2
, this is satisfied e.g. for
W\in\mathcal{S}_{W_{0}}(\underline{x}_{D})
. Then, following the notation from
Section

8
and
Definition

C.1
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}\penalty\ ,

where
h=0
is used in the definition of
\boldsymbol{A}
.

Proof

We first show
\boldsymbol{K}_{jk}=[\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}]_{jk}
for
j,k\in J
. Recall from
Section

8
and
Definition

C.1
that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&0&1&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{n^{\prime}}&0&1&0\\
0&x_{n^{\prime}+1}&0&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&x_{n}&0&1\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1,a^{2}}+\Sigma_{1,w^{2}}&0&\Sigma_{1,ab}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{-1,a^{2}}+\Sigma_{-1,a^{2}}&0&\Sigma_{-1,ab}\\
\Sigma_{1,ab}&0&1+\Sigma_{1,b^{2}}+\Sigma_{1,w^{2}}&1\\
0&\Sigma_{-1,ab}&1&1+\Sigma_{-1,b^{2}}+\Sigma_{-1,w^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ .

We consider several cases:

•

Case 1:
x_{j}>0
and
x_{k}<0
. Then, since no neuron is activated for both
x_{j}
and
x_{k}
,

\displaystyle K_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\left(\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial a_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial a_{i}}+\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial b_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial b_{i}}+\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial w_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial w_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial c}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0+1\cdot 1=1=\begin{pmatrix}x_{j}\\
0\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
x_{k}\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}=[(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}]_{jk}\penalty\ .

•

Case 2:
x_{j},x_{k}>0
. Then,

\displaystyle K_{jk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\left(\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial a_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial a_{i}}+\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial b_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial b_{i}}+\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial w_{i}}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial w_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{j})}{\partial c}\frac{\partial f_{W}(x_{k})}{\partial c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\left(w_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}+w_{i}^{2}+(a_{i}x_{j}+b_{i})(a_{i}x_{k}+b_{i})\right)+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1,w^{2}}(x_{j}x_{k}+1)+\Sigma_{1,a^{2}}x_{j}x_{k}+\Sigma_{1,ab}(x_{j}+x_{k})+\Sigma_{1,b^{2}}+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x_{j}\\
0\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\begin{pmatrix}x_{k}\\
0\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=[(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}]_{jk}\penalty\ .

•

Case 3:
x_{j},x_{k}<0
. This can be handled just like Case 2.

The identity
\mathbf{M}_{D}=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}
is easy to verify using the definitions of
\mathbf{M}_{D}
and
\boldsymbol{X}
.

It remains to show the last identity. For
j\in J
and
\sigma=\sgn(x_{j})
, we have

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{v})_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{\sigma}x_{j}+q_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:deriv:linear_relations}}}}{{=}}f_{W,\boldsymbol{\tau},\sigma}(x_{j})=f_{W}(x_{j})\penalty\ .

Therefore,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}f_{W}(x_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f_{W}(x_{n})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}\penalty\ .

(25)

Using
Definition

C.1
and
Definition

8
, we also obtain

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n\mathbf{M}_{D})^{-1}=(n\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{D}\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}})^{-1}=\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{D}^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{D}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{D,1}\\
&\mathbf{M}_{D,-1}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{D,1}^{-1}\\
&\mathbf{M}_{D,-1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j\in J_{1}}x_{j}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{-1}}x_{j}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{1}}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{-1}}y_{j}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{n}\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j\in J_{1}}x_{j}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{1}}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{-1}}x_{j}y_{j}\\
\sum_{j\in J_{-1}}y_{j}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{0}_{D,1}\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{0}_{D,-1}\end{pmatrix}

and therefore

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{D,1}^{-1}\\
&\mathbf{M}_{D,-1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{0}_{D,1}\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}^{0}_{D,-1}\end{pmatrix}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{D,1}\\
\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{D,-1}\end{pmatrix}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\mathrm{opt}}

(26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{opt}}\penalty\ .

Subtracting (
26
) from (
25
) yields the desired identity

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\penalty\ .

∎

Table L.1:
For different combinations of data distribution, parameterization, optimizer and bias initialization, this table shows the mean RMSE over 100 runs and the estimated standard deviation of the mean estimator, multiplied by
10^{4}
and rounded to the nearest integer, respectively. Cell colors are interpolated on a logarithmic scale from white (best result in column) to red (worst result in column). The bias initialization methods He+5 and
X_{k-}
and the distribution
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{\mathrm{ex}}
are explained in
Appendix

L
, while the other terms are explained in
Section

10
.

In
Table

L.1
, we provide the detailed experimental results for the plots shown in
Section

10
and some more results. In particular, we provide results for two other bias initializations:

•

Random point combination (
He+5
): This is one of the variants of the initialization methods proposed by
Steinwart 2019
, given by
b_{i}=-\sum_{k=1}^{5}\lambda_{k}\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{j_{k}}\rangle
, where
j_{k}
are random indices and
\lambda
is randomly drawn from a simplex.

•

Negative random point kink initialization (
X_{k-}
): Here, we choose
b_{i}=-\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}\rangle
, where
\boldsymbol{x}
is a random data point. If
b_{i}
is positive, we update
b_{i}\leftarrow-b_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\leftarrow-\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
, such that
b_{i}
becomes negative and the kink hyperplane does not change.

We also provide results for the data-generating distribution
P^{\mathrm{data}}_{\mathrm{ex}}
used in
Figure

9
: We sample

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{196}{105}}\sigma\beta\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{0.727}\left(\cos\left(7\pi\left(x-\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{5}x^{2}+\frac{1}{10}\varepsilon+0.074\right)\penalty\ ,

where
\sigma\sim\mathcal{U}\{-1,1\}
,
\beta\sim\Beta(5,2)
and
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are independent. The factor
\sqrt{196/105}
is chosen such that
\Var(x)=1
. The parameter
5
in the Beta distribution ensures
\eta=5
in the sense of
17
. The distribution of
y
is designed to yield visually interesting results, make
y
approximately normalized and ensure
q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{P^{\mathrm{data}},\pm 1}\approx 0
.
